### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 歐洲央行上周四(9/10)為年內第二次上調利率，以應對能源價格上漲帶來的通膨
壓力 ， 並警告物價壓力可能比先前預期更久 ， 推動市場加大對後續進一步收緊政
策的押注 。 歐洲央行總裁拉加德形容此次决定爲”無需多想的决定”， 並警告通膨
回落至 2%目標的時間點目前預計在 2027 年底。歐洲央行預期明年平均通膨爲
2.5%，高於 6 月的 2.3%；2027 年核心通膨預期爲 2.6%，高於此前的 2.5%。
⚫ 軍事消息人士稱，與伊朗結盟的胡塞武裝上周四(9/10)奪取葉門港口城市的控制
權 ， 並沿紅海海岸向南推進至戰略島嶼地區 。 美國總統川普表示 ， 他預計伊朗戰
爭將在美國中期選舉後結束。若胡塞武裝控制這條與 荷姆茲海峽同樣重要的水
道 ， 其支持者伊朗將在與美國的戰爭中獲得關鍵戰略優勢 ， 並進一步推高油價 。
⚫ 美國勞工部勞工統計局上週四(9/10)公布 8 月生産者物價指數 PPI 年增 5.4%，

高於市場預期的 5.3％。週五(9/11)公布 8 月消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 3.4%與前
月相同，核心 CPI 年增率 2.4%符合市場預期，月增率達 0.3%高於市場預期。
FED WATCH顯示數據公布後推升市場對於聯準會本周將升息的預期。
⚫ 日本央行數據顯示，8 月國內企業物價指數較去年同期上漲 7.6%。增幅高於市
場預估中值 7.4%。7 月漲幅經修正後爲 7.7%；8 月進口物價指數同比上漲 24.8%，
7 月修正後爲上漲 29.3%， 表明當月日圓貶值以及中東局勢影響推高進口成本和
整體通膨，同時鞏固市場對日本央行在 9 月會議進一步升息預期。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
美元兌新台幣匯率走勢
Sep/14/2026
＜上週走勢＞
上週台幣匯價高點為 31.455，低點在 31.698，以 31.638 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 0.8 分。
上週台幣先升後貶，週初，受到市場對於日本央行 9 月升息的預期升溫，日圓
走升帶動美元指數跌破 99 整數價位低檔震盪 ， 加以台股高檔震盪 ， 外資延續匯入的
走勢，帶動台幣升破31.500 至 31.455 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月生產者物
價指數年增率由上個月的 4.8%上升至 5.4%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示聯準會
9 月升息機率攀升至 7 成，美元指數回到 99 上方，台股高點回落，外資偏向匯出，
台幣由升轉貶至 31.698 週內低點，終場在央行調節之下收在 31.638。
＜未來分析＞
預估本週台幣區間震盪。國際市場方面，上週五公布美國 8 月核心消費者物價
指數月增率 0.3%高於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，提高本週聯準會升息的機率

至接近 9 成，此外中東局勢仍有升溫風險，油價持續高檔震盪，美元指數在 99 整數
關口盤整；就台灣市場來看，台灣 8 月出口衝上 824 億美元，創下歷年單月新高紀
錄，月增 9.4%，年增 41%，展現極為強勁的成長動能。顯示受惠於 AI 需求全面爆
發，帶動外銷出口暢旺，基本面強勁持續支撐台幣的表現；盤勢上，匯價逢低吸引
進口商及政府基金買盤進場，逢高則面臨出口商賣美金，且本週台美均有利率決策
會議，預估匯率波動將加劇。綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間震盪。
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.400-31.900

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/14/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週人民幣震盪走升。週初，雖然美伊戰火未平，帶動油價及美債利率高漲，
但市場對於日本央行 9 月升息預期升溫，日圓偏升帶動美元指數跌破 99 價位後低
檔震盪，人民幣延續偏升走勢，走升至6.7022 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月
生產者物價指數 5.4%優於市場預期 5.3%， 加以8 月核心消費者物價指數月增率 0.3%
優於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示本週聯準會 9 月升息預期大
幅攀升至近 9 成，美元指數回到99 上方，人民幣回吐部分週初以來的漲幅，終場境
外人民幣收在 6.7071，週線升值 8 點，境內人民幣則收在6.7079，週線升值 29 點。  
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣區間偏升。國際市場方面，美國通膨數據表現優於市場預期，
加以油價攀升至每桶 100 美元上方 ， 美債殖利率位居高檔 ， 美元指數也在99 上方盤
整，關注本週聯準會最新利率決議，預計在會議結果出爐以前，聯準會升息預期將
支撐美元指數，限制人民幣升值的空間。中國國內方面，人行中間價格緩步偏升，
上週升破 6.7800 價位，持續釋放人民幣偏強信號，支撐人民幣延續升值的方向。技
術面方面 ，USD/CNH 匯價居於各天期均線下方 ，且均線向下發散 ，加以匯價由低點
向上反彈測試 6.7150 關鍵價位後面臨壓力，顯示空方動能較盛，預計人民幣將延續
升值的態勢。綜合以上，預估本週人民幣走勢區間偏升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6800-6.7300

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 USD/JPY 震盪下跌 。週初 ，因市場預期日本央行將加快升息步伐及資金將
回流日本，使 USD/JPY 跌破至 155 整數關卡之下；週間，USD/JPY 延續跌勢最低
來到 152.87 後反彈，後續大致在 153 至 154 間波動；臨近週末，美國公布 8 月 PPI
及 CPI 數據顯示通膨仍處高位 ，USD/JPY 一度反彈至154.6 後回落 ， 終場收在153.54，
週線大跌 270 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週公布日本第二季 GDP
較上季增長 0.4%，季增年率也由 1.1%上升至 1.4%，主因為企業資本支出改善，而
7 月名目薪資年增 4.7%為 30 年來最大漲幅，而實質薪資年增 2.4%也為 5 年多來最
大漲幅，顯示日本經濟復甦力道較原先預期強勁，進一步強化市場對於日央升息的
預期，有利日圓表現。二、美國 8 月 CPI 及 PPI 數據顯示美國通膨仍處高位，強化
市場對聯準會升息預期，且中東局勢升溫，帶動油價突破 100 美元，預期將支撐美
元，限縮 USD/JPY 下行空間。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價多次向
下測試 153 整數關卡未能有效突破，顯示下方有買盤支撐，而 155 關卡附近則為前
波壓力位，因此短線先以區間看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的
可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：152.00-157.00 
前週交易區間 
152.87 156.27

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 EUR/USD 小幅下跌。 週初 ，EUR/USD 大致在 1.1600 至 1.1635 窄幅波動；
週間，市場等待歐洲央行利率決議及 美國通膨數據，EUR/USD 持續在 1.1615 至
1.1653 波動；週四晚間公布歐洲央行利率決議如預期升息 1 碼，而美國 8 月 PPI 高
於預期使市場對聯準會本月升息預期上升，EUR/USD 下行跌破 1.16 整數關口；臨
近週末，美國公布 8 月 CPI 顯示通膨仍具韌性，帶動 EUR/USD 走低來至 1.1568，
終場收在 1.1598，週線下跌 15 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週歐洲央行公布利率決
議如預期升息一碼，總裁拉加德表示即使面對能源價格衝擊，歐洲經濟第二季依然
展現韌性，預期第三季仍會延續，但未來通膨仍面臨上行風險，偏鷹派言論使市場
對於歐洲央行未來持續升息的預期上升，將帶給歐元支撐。二、美國 8 月 PPI 月增
0.4%符合市場預期 ， 年增5.4%高於預期 ，而 8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%年增 3.4%符合預期 ，
顯示通膨仍維持高位，使市場對聯準會升息預期增加，將支撐美元，不利歐元匯價
上行。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖一度突破 1.1630 年線但未
能站穩，顯示上方仍有賣壓，而下方1.1550 附近則有支撐，在有效突破前以區間震
盪看待。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1530-1.1680 
前週交易區間 
1.1568 1.1653

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                         新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.07-115.09.11 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.66% ~ 1.69% 
公債－10 年期-A15106 1.92 % ~ 1.921% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.822% ~ 1.823%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ，本週央行連日減發定存單 ， 使市場資金呈現平穩態勢 ， 且
資金需求不若先前，銀行間交投較為清淡，令短率持穩於近期區間。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.69%，利率較上週上升 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週 5 年期指標券 A15108 成交在約 1.82%，10 年期指標券 A15106 成交在約
1.92%。 
美伊衝突升溫，帶動國際油價持續走揚，加劇市場對通膨之疑慮，加上美國8
月非農就業人數大增、8 月 PPI 年增率高於市場預期，市場預估聯準會下週升
息機率升至七成，帶動美債利率大幅反彈；台灣央行將於下週召開理監事會
議 ， 在台灣經濟成長與通膨維持高位下 ， 台灣央行面臨緊縮壓力 ， 預期台債殖
利率面臨走升壓力 ， 預估5 年券區間為 1.83%~1.90%，10 年券區間為 1.93%~2%。

產業速報  
                                        2026 年 10 月 14 日

1 群益投顧 
京元電 (2449 TT) 宣 布 與 NVIDIA  International, Inc. 簽訂預付款協議，支持美國全資
子公司 AeternZ 於 當 地 建 置 產 能 ， 但 現 階 段 仍 未 揭 露 合 作 金 額。 NVIDIA 於 07/2026
與 Amkor (AMKR US) 也 簽 約 相 似 的 預 付 款 合 約。顯 示 AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片
(SoC) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展 ，封 測 複 雜 度 提 升 ，IC 設 計 廠 和 封 測 廠 合 作 深 化，
提 高 後 段 封 測 廠 訂 單 能 見 度 。 不 過 和NVIDIA 合作的此協議短期效益有限，京元電評
價合理 。

研 究 員 ： 陳 俐 妍   
ly.c@capital.com.tw  
京 元 電 宣 布 與 NVIDIA 簽 訂 預 付 款 協 議 ， 支 持 美 國 全 資 子 公 司 AeternZ 於
當 地 建 置 產 能，但 現 階 段 仍 未 揭 露 合 作 金 額：  
10/13/2026 京 元 電 重 訊 公 告 ， 董 事 會 決 議 通 過 與NVIDIA(NVDA US) 簽 訂 客
戶 預 付 款 協 議，將 由 NVIDIA 預 先 支 付 款 項，支 持 京 元 電 及 美 國 全 資 子 公 司
AeternZ Semiconductor Testing Corporation 在 美 國 建 置 測 試 產 能 ， 未 來 再 以
提 供 測 試 服 務 抵 減 預 付 款。京 元 電 同 步 宣 布，因 合 約 架 構 調 整，取 消 原 先 對
美 國 子 公 司 提 供 的 3 億 美 元 背 書 保 證 額 度 。 
京 元 電 此 次簽約 對 象 為 NVIDIA International, Inc. ， NVIDIA 預 付 款 項 ， 京
元 電 及 其 美 國 全 資 子 公 司 將 在 美 國 建 置 測 試 產 能 ， 為 NVIDIA 保 留 產 能 並
提 供 測 試 服 務，預 付 款 則 以 未 來 服 務 款 項 抵 減。此 合 作 並 非 股 權 投 資 或 合 資
模 式，而 是 在 既 有 客 戶 關 係 下，透 過 客 戶 提 前 提 供 資 金，支 持 供 應 商 擴 充 產
能 並 確 保 未 來 測 試 資 源 。 
京 元 電 董 事 會 已 於 07/ 2026 決 議 通 過 美 國 投 資 設 廠 案，規 劃 在 美 國 設 立 孫 公
司 並 興 建 新 廠 ， 投 資 金 額 以 14 億美元 (約 新 台 幣 450 億元 )為 上 限 。 公 司 當
時 表 示，此 投 資 主 要 因 應 未 來 營 運 需 求 及 全 球 供 應 鏈 布 局，後 續 將 依 設 廠 進
度 與 資 金 需 求 逐 步 投 入 ， 資 金 來 源 以 自 有 資 金 或 借 款 等 方 式 支 應 。 
此 次 協 議 有 助 於 提 高 新 增 產 能 的 需 求 能 見 度，並 降 低 部 分 初 期 資 金 壓 力。不
過，由 於 預 付 款 金 額、實 際 保 留 產 能 規 模 及 相 關 訂 單 金 額 尚 未 公 開，現 階 段
仍 無 法 量 化 對 營 收 與 獲 利 的 貢 獻；短 期 營 運 表 現 仍 取 決 於 既 有 產 能 擴 充、新
晶 片 測 試 需 求 及 美 國 廠 建 置 進 度 。 
NVIDIA 在 07/2026 已和 Amkor(AMKR US) 簽 訂 預 付 款 協 議 ， 約 15 億美
元 ：  
值 得 注 意 的 是 ，NVIDIA 於 07/ 2026 與 Amkor 簽訂約 15 億 美 元 的 多 年 期 先
進 封 裝 與 測 試 合 作 協 議，包 括 正 在 亞 利 桑 那 州 建 設 的 生 產 設 施。Amkor 目前
已為 NVIDIA 資 料 中 心 處 理 器 、 網 通 晶 片 組 及 加 速 運 算 系 統 提 供 先 進 封 裝
服務 。  
Amkor 於 2Q 26 法 說 會 表 示 ， 相 關 預 付 款 預 計 於 2027 年 收 取 ， 並 隨 後 續 提
供 服 務 逐 步 抵 減 ， 依 實 際 生 產 規 模 及 服 務 提 供 速 度 ， 預 估 合 作 期 間 約5~10
年 。 此 案 例 顯 示 ，NVIDIA 透 過 預 付 款 支 持 封 測 廠 擴 充 美 國 產 能 ， 並 非 短 期
採 購 安 排 ， 而 可 能 是 配 合 長 期 產 能 建 置 及 技 術 發 展 的 合 作 模 式 。 
京 元 電 此 次 亦 採 取和 Amkor 相 似 的 方 式 ，客 戶 預 付 款 、 建 置 美 國 產 能 並 以
未 來 測 試 服 務 款 項 抵 減。 Amkor 案 例 可 作 為 評 估 合 作 期 間 的 參 考。  
2 家 不 同 之 處，京 元 電 主 要 受 惠 於 高 階 測 試 需 求；Amkor 則 涉 及 先 進 封 裝 與

### Page 2

2 群益投顧 
測 試。兩 者 都 能 提 高 產 能 需 求 能 見 度，但 實 際 營 收 與 獲 利 貢 獻 仍 取 決 於 產 能
開 出 、 訂 單 規 模 及 服 務 條 件 。 
外 媒 報 導 京 元 電 美 國 子 公 司預 計 落 腳 德 州 沃 斯 堡 市(Fort Worth )：  
在 美 國 設 廠 地 點 方 面， 外 媒 於10/ 2026 報 導， 京 元 電 全 資 美 國 子 公 司AeternZ
正 評 估 於 德 州 沃 斯 堡(Fort Worth )設 立 半 導 體 測 試 廠，初 步 規 劃 投 資 約 13 億
美 元 ， 其 中 約 10 億 美 元 用 於 不 動 產 、3 億 美 元 用 於 設 備 ， 選 址 涉 及 West 
Everman Parkway 一 處 約 23 英 畝 的 土 地 。 沃 斯 堡 市 議 會 預 計 於 10 /27/2026
審 議 相 關 租 稅 優 惠，但 目 前 尚 不 能 視 為 京 元 電 已 正 式 決 定 設 廠 地 點，仍 須 觀
察 後 續 選 址 及 投 資 進 度。若 該 地 點 獲 選，德州相對 台 積 電 所 在 的 亞 利 桑 那 州
鳳 凰 城 聚 落 ，地 緣 上 相 對 鄰 近 AI 伺 服 器 供 應 鏈 ，代 表 AI 晶 片 完 成 測 試 後，
可 就 近 送 往 當 地 代 工 廠 ， 進 行 整 機 組 裝 與 系 統 測 試 。不 過 ， 廠 房 建 置 、 設 備
進 駐 及 客 戶 認 證 仍 需 時 間 ， 實 際 貢 獻 時 點 取 決 於 建 廠 進 度 與 測 試 需 求 。 
AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片(SoC) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展， 封 測 複 雜
度 提 升 ， IC 設 計 廠 和 封 測 廠 合 作 深 化：  
隨著 AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片(SoC ) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展 ， 半 導
體 產 業 的 競 爭 已 不 再 侷 限 於 晶 片 設 計 與 先 進 製 程，也 延 伸 至 先 進 封 裝、晶 粒
間 高 速 互 連、散 熱 及 測 試 驗 證 等 環 節。晶 片 架 構 複 雜 度 提 高，使 封 裝 與 測 試
成 為 影 響 產 品 效 能 、 成 本 及 量 產 能 力 的 重 要 因 素 。 
對 京 元 電 而 言，若 能 持 續 參 與 NVIDIA 新 晶 片 的 測 試 開 發 與 驗 證，並 承 接 後
續 量 產 測 試，有 助 於 深 化 客 戶 關 係 及 鞏 固 高 階 測 試 業 務 的 競 爭 優 勢；但 此 次
預付款協議本身並未改變雙方的股權關係，合作價值仍須透過後續訂單與
產 能 利 用 率 驗 證 。 
相 較 之 下 ， 力 成(6239 TT) 與 Broadcom (AVGO US) 規 劃 在 新 加 坡 成 立 先 進 封
裝 合 資 公 司 ， 發 展 扇 出 型 面 板 級 封 裝 （FOPLP ）及 重 布 線 層 （RDL ） 等 相 關
技術，屬於 IC 設 計 公 司 直 接 參 與 封 裝 製 造 平 台 投 資 的 模 式 。 此 合 作 不 僅 涉
及 產 能 配 置，也 涵 蓋 新 技 術 開 發 與 量 產 導 入，反 映 當 先 進 封 裝 成 為 影 響 晶 片
效 能 、 互 連 密 度 及 量 產 成 本 的 關 鍵 環 節 ，IC 設 計 公 司 可 能 更 積 極 參 與 封 裝
技 術 與 產 能 布 局。對 力 成 而 言，若 能 順 利 完 成 製 程 驗 證、良 率 提 升 及 量 產 導
入，有 機 會 拓 展 新 的 技 術 平 台 與 業 務 成 長 機 會；惟 仍 須 觀 察 客 戶 訂 單、量 產
進 度 、 資 本 支 出 及 投 資 報 酬 率 。 
整 體 而 言 ，NVIDIA 與 京 元 電 、Amkor 的 合 作 ， 主 要 是 透 過 客 戶 預 付 款 支 持
產 能 建 置 ， 強 化 供 應 商 的 需 求 與 資 金 能 見 度 ；Broadcom 與 力 成 的 合 作 ， 則
是 透 過 共 同 投 資 發 展 新 的 先 進 封 裝 技 術 平 台 。 兩 者 反 映AI 半 導 體 產 業 的 合
作 模 式 正 逐 步 深 化，但 資 金 安 排、技 術 參 與 程 度 及 投 資 風 險 並 不 相 同。投 資
上，京 元 電 應 關 注 預 付 款 規 模、美 國 新 廠 投 產 及 高 階 測 試 訂 單 成 長；力 成 則
應 觀 察 先 進 封 裝 技 術 商 業 化、量 產 良 率 與 合 資 事 業 的 投 資 報 酬。京 元 電 此 次
協 議 對 中 長 期 業 務 發 展 具 正 面 意 義，但 在 實 際 財 務 貢 獻 尚 未 明 朗 前，宜 視 為
客 戶 關 係 深 化 及 產 能 布 局 的 正 面 訊 號，而 非 直 接 上 修 獲 利 預 估 的 依 據。由於
目 前 京 元 電 評 價 合 理 ，投 資 建 議 Neutral 。

產業速報

4 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

研 究  報 告                
晶呈科技(4768.TT) 
產業：化學工業 
氣體原料量產降本效應與玻璃加工產品放量，推動明年獲利高成⻑ 
 投資評價與建議 
考量三廠C4F6⼀級合成放量顯著優化⽑利率重回30%，且AI驅動TGV及CPO
加工產品步入規模化量產，⻑期動能強勁，參考台股半導體特化與先進封裝概
念股本益比30-35倍，因短期受高額研發費用投入、新廠折舊與轉虧為盈初期
致使當前本益比失真，故採用TGV產線滿載Forward PER。 
 重點評論分析 
特氣出貨回升，TGV量產放量：推估4Q26隨特氣出貨回升及客⼾去庫存告⼀
段落，營收可望季增73.7%至6.6億元，全年營收估增50.9%至18.6億元，玻璃
加工受惠AI高階封裝與CPO需求，GCB及One Piece Glass FAU已進入規模量
產，預估2H26營收較上半年成⻑230%，惟三廠延至4Q26試量產，特氣原料
成本改善遞延，⽑利率下修至23%，加上研發費用增加，使稅後EPS 0.29元。  
⼀級合成與高⽑利產品帶動獲利躍升： 預估2027年營收年增35.1%至25.1億
元，受惠AI ASIC大廠特氣需求放量、TGV及矽光子共同封裝相關應用擴產6倍
產能，並拓展生醫檢測市場，預計3Q27末相關產線陸續量產，Stripper隨認證
客⼾增加至9家，⽑利率因三廠C4F6⼀級合成與純化工廠預計1Q27放量，年
產能250噸，可降低外購原料成本，加上Stripper產能利用率提升，以及TGV、
生醫檢測及Stripper等高⽑利產品營收占比提升至20%，帶動年增7.92ppts至
30.9% ， 營益率升至12.5% ， 稅前盈餘年增936%至2.91億元 ， 稅後EPS 4.30元 。  
 投資風險 
⼀級合成新廠量產進度與高⽑利產品TGV 、 特化投產或客⼾導入進度不如預期 。

### Page 2

2 
| 公司簡介  
公司所做的玻璃通孔技術(Through Glass Via ,TGV)主要應用於大積體電路玻璃載板(Glass Core Su bstrate, 
GCS)(非 Glass interposer)，是做為⼀個玻璃核心晶圓加工廠(TGV Glass Foundry)，並將加工好 的玻璃核心層
(Glass Core Board, GCB)，交付給下游 IC 載板廠或封測廠後，在玻璃核心的外側壓合 ABF 絕緣膜，並製作精細
的銅線路 ， 組合成完整的 GCS ． 亦透過玻璃加工技術開發⼀體成型式 CPO 光纖束導基座(One Piece Glass FAU)。 
GCB 是數個晶片叢共用的超大玻璃地基，支撐所有晶片和並保持封裝的平坦和穩定，解決穩定性(平坦度與結構支
撐)的瓶頸，位置在整個封裝體的最底層，玻璃材料解決熱膨脹、曲蹺度、以及對封裝尺寸的要求極限，目前公司
的製作玻璃微孔的貫穿深度與孔道寬度(孔徑)的比值已達到深寬比 100:1，全孔立體型態增圓度可達 99%、板厚
可達 1.85mm，單位面積產值相較 Glass Interposer 多 25-30 倍，在硬脆且易碎的玻璃材質上實現百倍深寬比，
是公司透過獨家氣態分解融蝕技術，在微觀垂直孔道上的極致控制力。 
One Piece Glass FAU 是⼀種應用於 CPO(共同封裝光學)與超高速光通訊系統中光電整合模組，超微細光纖束的
精準對位組裝，專為插裝與固定直徑僅 125 微米(µm)的超微細光纖束所設計(直徑極細，大約三根光纖才等於⼀
根頭髮的粗細)，在 AI 晶片架構從傳統電訊號傳輸轉向光訊號傳輸的過程中，該產品主要解決(1)微米級光纖對位
極難與光訊號損耗問題，公司雷射改質與氣態蝕刻技術(LADY)，在玻璃上打造出高⼀致性的微孔結構，提供高精
度的光纖對位，顯著提高光耦合效率(Optical Coupling Efficiency)，可減少多件式組裝產生的累積公差(2)高溫運
轉下的熱膨脹與光軸偏斜，利用玻璃材料極低熱膨脹係數的物理特性，確保光纖對位結構在⻑期高溫運算下仍保
持極佳的尺寸，(3)光軸穩定性以及光電整合元件的結構精密性，採用⼀體成型(One-Piece)的玻璃基座設計，結合
TGV 製程的高垂直度與高精度特性，配合客⼾在 AI 系統架構升級中對光電整合元件的規模化部署需求。 
圖表、Glass Core Board (左圖)不同象限與One Piece Glass FAU(右圖)顯微鏡下示意圖

| 營  運  概  況  
外銷與特氣出貨拉動累計營業收入倍增，受費用提升致⽑利率承壓：1H26 合併營收 8.21 億元，YoY+141%，主
要受惠特殊氣體外銷出口的大幅增加，氣體出貨支數達 13,022 支，其中 2Q26 營收 QoQ-13.9%至 3.8 億元，主
因在於部分外銷出口出貨量有所減少至 6,294 支，受韓國等記憶體客⼾要求調整出貨影響，但⽑利率
QoQ+11.1ppts 至 24.4%，因低⽑利產品(海外記憶體客⼾的特氣)相對 1Q26 出貨較少，1H26 ⽑利率 YoY-
20.45ppts 至 18.5%，除了產品組合關係，另因上游⼀級原料合成⾃給率尚未開出，使得原料成本負擔較沉重，
2Q26 費用率從 17.6%提升至 25.6%，主要來⾃研究發展費用大幅提升從 7.1%提升到 12.2%，目的在於為竹北五
廠、頭份三廠等新廠建置進行關鍵技術研發，1H26 稅前虧損 0.36 億元，稅後 EPS -0.99 元。 
特氣與 TGV 放量帶動 2H26 營收優於 1H26：3Q26 單季營收 3.8 億元，ＱoQ-0.6%，推估 3Q26 鋼瓶出貨量
6,000-6,200 支，主要來⾃ C4F6 內外銷出貨持續，⽑利率維持 24%，推估 4Q26 營收 QoQ+73.7%至 6.6 億元， 
4Q26 鋼瓶出貨量 7,500-7,800 支，2H26 鋼瓶出貨合計約 13,500-14,000 支，比 1H26 出貨量成⻑約 7.7%，隨
著全球半導體大廠的新產能與新廠陸續開出，帶動對氣體 C4F6 蝕刻氣體、Laser Gas 等的剛性需求持續增加，另

### Page 3

3 
外 2H26 隨客⼾去庫存化使需求回補，帶動氣體鋼瓶出貨量提升，並受惠於 AI 伺服器與高階封裝和矽光子共同封
裝(Co-Packaged Optics, CPO)，使玻璃加工市場快速成⻑，帶動相關特殊氣體的需求同步提升。 
玻璃加工技術在 TGV Glass Foundry 領域應用，已成功建置兩大可量產的核心產品線，配合年底 2 條產線(月產
能約 1 萬片)到位與量產放量，預估 2H26 營收相較 1H26 成⻑ 230%，主因為 GCB 與 One Piece Glass FAU 皆
已跨越工程驗證，正式進入單月接單與出貨片數達四位數以上的規模量產的放量階段，預估單片 150x150mm(5
萬孔/片)價值約$500-700 美元。 
特化產品 Stripper 目前設有⼀條年產能 288 頓產線，目前已被客⼾認證達 15 個品項，其中 7 個已達穩定交貨，
驗證通過的客⼾數已增加至９家，預估 2H26 營收相較 1H26 成⻑ 3%，加計其他產品，預估 2026 全年營收成⻑
率下修至 50.9%達 18.5 億元 ， 由於三廠(C4F6 ⼀級合成工廠)投產時程 ， 從先前 3Q26 延後至 4Q26 進行試量產 ，
使全年⽑利率從原先預估的 35%下修至 23%，加上研發費用擴張，下修稅前盈餘年成⻑ 41.3%至 0.28 億元，稅
後E P S  0 . 2 9元 。  
⼀級合成放量大幅優化特氣成本結構，TGV 導入 AI 大廠放量，帶動獲利大幅成⻑：邁入 2027 年，預估全年營收
成⻑ 35.1%至 25.1 億元，主要成⻑來⾃特殊氣體核心業務持續受惠 AI ASIC 大廠需求放量，另外玻璃加工應用於
AI 先進封裝與矽光子共同封裝產品預計於 3Q27 末累計建置達 12 條產線並正式量產，推動營收規模呈指數級成
⻑，在生醫檢測應用(竹北五廠)的最新進程上，已於生醫園區簽約購入竹北五廠房，將玻璃加工技術應用於生產生
醫用檢測儀器，在玻璃培養⽫微雕出高⼀致性的 3D 微孔陣列，模擬人體內真實的立體腫瘤微環境(3D 全立體型
態載具(Booster))，針對 EGFR 基因突變陽性的局部晚期非小細胞肺癌細胞進行精準控制併用標靶藥物泰格沙
(Tagrisso®)之細胞檢測實驗體外評估(In Vitro) ， 提升藥物對癌細胞的抑制與殺傷成效 ， 並藉由開發的生醫檢測儀
器，作為癌症精準用藥的關鍵數據，預計五廠將於 3Q27 末正式開始量產，此應用將使玻璃加工技術跨足高⽑利
生醫市場，現有廠房空間至少可規劃 6 條產線，後續將視客⼾需求進行滾動擴充。Stripper 隨 2026 年認證客⼾
增加朝向產能滿載，以及二廠無水氟化氫(AHF)量產線與三氯化硼(BCl3)試產線裝機完成，四條產線年產值上看 3-
4 億元的效益下，穩定貢獻營收。2027 全年⽑利率則受惠頭份三廠的 C4F6 ⼀級合成與純化工廠預計於 1Q27 正
式放量，設計年產能 250 噸，特氣⾃給率將大幅拉升，改變過去依賴外購二、三級純化原料的成本結構，使成本
大幅降低，加上特用化學品業務在產能利用率拉升，高⽑利產品如 TGV 玻璃載板、生醫檢測儀器、Stripper ⾃製
放量的佔比合計提升至 20%，使⽑利率顯著提升 7.92ppts 至 30.9%，費用率隨營業收入規模擴大而稀釋至 18.36%，
營益率提升 9.75ppt 至 12.5%，稅前盈餘年成⻑ 936%至 2.91 億元，稅後 EPS 4.30 元。

### Page 1

삼성바이오로직스 (207940/KS) 
3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 
2026-10-06

제약/바이오. 이선경 / seonkyoung.lee@sks.co.kr / 3773-9089

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 2,000,000 원(유지) 
현재주가: 1,354,000 원 
상승여력: 47.7%

Signal: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 전망  
Key: 5 공장 매출 본격화, 미국 공장 매출 기여에 따른 성장 기대   
Step: 단기 불확실성보다는 중장기적 성장 방향성에 주목

3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 성장지속, 컨센 부합 전망   
26년 3분기 연결기준 매출액은 1조 3,741억원(+9.3%YoY, +4.0%QoQ), 영업이
익은 6,098억원(-3.7%YoY, +4.0%QoQ, OPM 44.4%)으로 컨센서스 매출액 1
조 3,450억원과 영업이익 6,063억원에 부합하는 실적을 전망한다.  
5월 파업 영향으로 약 20개 배치 생산분(약 1,500억원)에 대한 원가 손실이 인식되
고 3분기 평균 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락했음에도 불구하고, 3분기부터 매출로 
인식되는 5공장 PPQ 배치 및 미국 공장의 매출 기여로 지속적인 성장이 예상된다. 약 
1,500억원 규모의 생산 차질분은 고객사와 일정 조율을 통해 출하될 예정으로 이에 
따른 4분기 매출 성장을 기대해 볼 수 있다.   
신규 수주 회복과 모달리티 확장에 주목 
시장에서 우려했던 신규 수주는 관세 이슈로 고객사의 컨택이 감소하면서 올해 저조했
으나 4 월 Section 232 결과 발표 이후 논의가 회복 단계에 있어 연내 추가 신규 수주 
가능성은 열려 있다.  
항체 시장의 경쟁심화와 차세대 모달리티 확장에 대응하기 위해 결정한 폴리펩타이드
의 인수는 연내 마무리될 예정이다. 폴리펩타이드의 2028 년 목표 EBITDA 마진은 
25%로 동사 대비 낮은 상황이나, 펩타이드 기반 CDMO 시장 성장에 따른 마진 개선
도 가능하다. 현재 임단협, 환율, 미국 공장 및 폴리펩타이드 인수에 따른 회계 영향 등 
불확실성이 큰 상황이나, 수주 회복과 5 공장 매출 인식 본격화, 미국 공장 및 멀티 모
달리티 확장 등 중장기 성장 방향성에 주목할 필요가 있다.

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 우려 감안해도 충분히 매력적HD현대중공업(329180) 우려 감안해도 충분히 매력적우려 감안해도 충분히 매력적 - 기업.pdf

### Page 1

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
우려 감안해도 충분히 매력적 
2026-10-14

조선/방산. 한승한, R.A 고서영 / shane.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-9992

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 820,000 원(하향) 
현재주가: 366,500 원 
상승여력: 123.7%

Signal: 3Q26, 조업일수 및 환율 영향으로 시장 예상치 하회 예상 
Key: 4Q26부터 본격적인 발전엔진 및 FDC 계약 수주 모멘텀 기대 
Step: 상선 우려 감안해도 4 행정 엔진&FDC으로 주가 상승여력 
충분. 매수

3Q26 Preview: 컨센서스 하회하는 실적 기록 예상 
3Q26 연결 매출액 6조 464억원(+36.9% YoY, -4.5% QoQ), 영업이익 9,230억
원(+65.6% YoY, -11.2% QoQ)으로, 시장 예상치(1조 70억원)를 하회하는 실적 기
록할 것으로 추정한다. 이전 분기 대비 중대재해 및 하계휴가, 그리고 추석 연휴에 따른 
조업일수 감소 효과와 더불어 큰 폭의 환율 하락 영향(약 500억원) 때문이다. 홍범도
함 화재 및 임단협 관련 일회성 비용은 이번 분기가 아닌 4Q 에 반영될 것으로 예상한
다. 해양 부문에서 트리온 FPU 프로젝트 C/O에 따른 일부 환입 발생한 것으로 추정
되며, 해양 도크에서 컨테이너선 선체 블록 일부 건조로 고정비 감소효과 일부 상쇄 중
이다. 다만 올해 연내 혹은 내년 초 추가 해양 수주 없을 시 고정비 상승 우려 확대될 
전망이다. 동사 올해 상선 수주 목표(114.7억불)를 이미 초과하는 수주 실적 기록한 상
황이며, 고마진 선종인 LNGC 및 VLGC/VLAC 중심으로 수주했기 때문에 ‘28~’29
년 구간 경쟁사 대비 뚜렷한 선종 믹스 효과를 기대한다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 유지, 목표주가 82 만원으로 하향 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’를 유지하고, 목표주가는 기존 97 만원에서 82
만원으로 하향한다. 중국의 공격적인 캐파 증설 지속과 더불어 중국 대형 조선사들의 
글로벌 선주가 발주한 LNGC 물량 및 원가 경쟁력 확대에 따른 상선 부문의 멀티플 
조정이 불가피하다는 판단이다. 하지만 상선 부문의 우려를 감안하더라도 상선 사이클
과 무관한 육상 발전 엔진 및 FDC에 대한 글로벌 하이퍼스케일러들의 수요 확대는 지
속되고 있는 중으로 파악된다. 올해 4 분기부터 4 행정 중속엔진&FDC 부문의 본격적
인 수주를 통해 중장기적 신성장 동력 확보할 전망이며, 해당 모멘텀에 따른 주가 상승
여력 충분히 남아있다는 판단으로, 조선주 최선호주(Top-Pick)를 유지한다.

Document 227: AI交易紧张情绪暂缓，美股指转涨，CoreWeave涨超5%，礼来涨近5%股价创新高，美元走软

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI交易紧张情绪暂缓，美股指转涨，CoreWeave涨超5%，礼来涨近5%股价创新高，美元走软.pdf

### Page 1

CoreWeave ⼀度涨超 5.3% ，美股盘初曾跌超 6.36% 。道琼斯指数涨超 1% ，标普 500 指数
涨 0.6% ，纳斯达克综合指数上涨 0.5% ，基准 KBW 银⾏指数上涨 2.0% ，摩根⼤通涨幅超过
1% 。
摘要：
标普 500 指数转涨，⼤型银⾏和医疗保健股领涨，纳斯达克 100 指数⼩幅反弹。不过，随着
Meta 、特斯拉等科技巨头本周陆续公布财报，投资者对 AI 投资热潮能否延续仍存疑虑，市场情
绪整体谨慎。
周⼆，纳斯达克指数上涨 0.5% ，基准 KBW 银⾏指数上涨 2.0% ，摩根⼤通涨幅超过 1% 。布伦特
原油从盘中⾼点回落，美国 10 年期国债收益率也有所下⾏。
科技股财报密集发布之际，市场对 AI 投资回报和相关资产估值的担忧持续发酵。数据中⼼基础设
施公司 Elice Inc. 在⾸尔上市⾸⽇股价暴跌逾 33% ，进⼀步打击了投资者对新兴云服务概念股的
信⼼。与此同时， SpaceX 和 Netflix 将在盘后公布业绩，成为市场关注的焦点。

富国银⾏分析师迈克 · 梅奥在报告中指出，抛售初期恐慌情绪占据上⻛，但市场反应可能过度。
他认为，相关银⾏股仍具备持续增⻓潜⼒，预期市盈率仅为 10 倍，显著低于标普 500 指数的 19
倍，这⼀估值差距有望推动市场情绪逐步回归理性。
财报季成 AI 交易关键考验
CoreWeave ⼀度涨超 5.3% ，美股盘初曾跌超 6.36% 。礼来涨近 5% ，股价创历史新⾼。
存储芯⽚股普跌， SK 海⼒⼠跌约 3% ，西部数据跌约 1% ，闪迪跌约 2% ，美光科技跌约 2% ，希
捷科技跌约 1% 。光通信股普跌，迈威尔科技跌约 2% ，康宁跌约 2% ， Coherent 跌约 4% ， Credo
跌约 3% ， Lumentum 跌约 3% 。
标普 500 指数低开 11.72 点，跌幅 0.15% ，报 7679.01 点；道琼斯⼯业平均指数低开 6.82 点，跌
幅 0.01% ，报 51959.21 点；
纳斯达克综合指数低开 97.97 点，跌幅 0.37% ，报 26332.92 点。

⽇经 225 指数收跌 1.3% ，报 64075.42 点。⽇本东证指数收跌 1.4% ，报 4009.21 点。韩国⾸尔
综指收跌 3.7 ，报 5552.74 点。
美元现货指数下跌 0.2%
10 年期美国国债收益率下降 2 个基点，⾄ 5.12%
⽇本 10 年期国债收益率上涨 1.5 个基点，⾄ 2.920%
欧元上涨 0.2% ，报 1.1231 美元
⻩⾦下跌 0.5% ，报每盎司约 4120 美元。
布伦特原油上涨⾄每桶 110.20 美元上⽅。

### Page 2

本周科技巨头财报密集落地，将直接检验 AI 相关股票涨势的成⾊。 Netflix 将于周⼆发布业绩，
Tesla 、 Meta 及 IBM Corp. 预计于隔⽇相继公布。
费城半导体指数周⼀上涨 1.6% ，终结了⾃ 2008 年以来持续时间最⻓的⼋连跌⾛势，带动纳斯达
克 100 指数从 4 ⽉关税动荡以来的最惨单周跌幅中有所回升。然⽽，标普 500 指数中仍约有 65% 的
成份股下跌，显⽰反弹缺乏⼴泛基础。 Pepperstone Group Ltd. 策略师 Dilin Wu 在报告中写
道： " 此次反弹主要由科技股驱动，⽽⾮⻛险偏好的全⾯回归。 "
在亚洲企业层⾯，彭博数据显⽰， SoftBank Group Corp. 的信⽤违约互换（ CDS ）利差已升⾄
近四年最⾼⽔平，反映市场对其 AI 相关融资及潜在⼤型并购的担忧情绪持续升温。
亚股与美股⾛势现分化
在美股技术反弹的提振下，亚太市场周⼀未能延续涨势，反映出投资者对 AI 交易前景的深层疑
虑。⽇经 225 指数收跌 1.3% ，报 64075.42 点；东证指数收跌 1.4% ，报 4009.21 点。

### Page 3

Nagomi Capital 投资管理主管 Kazuyuki Muramatsu 指出， Elice 上市即重挫， " 意味着新兴云端公
司所处的市场环境尚未完全改善 " ，对整体情绪构成拖累。他同时强调，市场能否真正企稳，还
需等待即将发布的财报加以验证。
彭博策略师 Mark Cranfield 亦指出，亚洲资产内部的分化⾛势令宏观投资者感到不安，这将进⼀
步拉⾼美国财报季、尤其是科技巨头业绩的市场期待。
美元⾛软，市场观望财报结果

### Page 4

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
美元兑所有 G10 货币均告下跌。澳⼤利亚联邦银⾏策略师 Carol Kong 指出，投资者不愿在科技巨
头财报发布前建⽴⼤仓位。 " ⻛险情绪和全球增⻓前景如今与 AI 交易深度绑定，投资者很可能在
等待财报结果出炉后，再重新评估美元仓位。 "
与此同时，美国国债收益率在周⼀因避险需求减退⽽⾛⾼后，周⼆有所回落。⻩⾦下跌 0.5% ，
报每盎司约 4120 美元。布伦特原油因伊朗在霍尔⽊兹海峡发动进⼀步袭击的⻛险升温，⽽波斯
湾原油流量整体维持稳定，油价在两股⼒量拉锯下上涨⾄每桶 110.20 美元上⽅。
法国政局动荡令欧元承压
欧元⾛势亦受到市场密切关注。法国⽣活成本抗议活动于周末演变为暴⼒冲突，引发⼤规模破
坏和抢劫事件，促使当局加强安保部署并承诺向受损企业提供援助。然⽽，法国政府背负沉重

债务负担，预算⾚字为欧元区最⾼之列，财政空间极为有限，难以通过⼤幅增⽀化解社会⽭
盾。
Generali Investments 宏观与市场研究主管 Thomas Hempell 在报告中写道： " 欧元多头如今寥寥
⽆⼏。伊朗战事持续、法国政局悬⽽未决，欧元短期内反弹的可能性不⼤。 "

Document 282: De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-09-15
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
De-reservification, Not De-dollarization
The world isn’t moving away from the dollar. It is shifting its dollars from traditional
reserves to state banks, pension funds, and other quasi-sovereign investors.
(Bernardo Montoya/Courtesy Reuters)
PUBLISHED
September 15, 2026 8:37 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
This is a joint blog with Alex Etra, Senior Macro
Strategist and head of Flow of Funds at Vanda
Research.
The IMF’s data on the currency composition of
foreign exchange (FX) reserves is scrutinized carefully
for any signs that the world is shifting way from the
dollar.
Yet it doesn’t really matter that much if the dollar’s
share of reserves has shifted from 56.5 percent to 57
percent. The ink-to-impact ratio of the quarterly

reporting on the latest COFER data is all off.
Neither the stock of global reserves nor the stock of
dollar reserves has changed much in the last ten years.
The action is elsewhere.

### Page 3

3
China’s state banks (per the Bank of International
Settlements) have almost as many foreign assets as the
central bank (PBOC).
Japan’s Government Pension Investment Fund
(GPIF) has almost as many foreign assets ($986
billion) as the government has FX reserves ($1.1
trillion at the end of August). Japan’s FX reserves are
on the books of the Ministry of Finance and the
GPIF is overseen by the Ministry of Health, Welfare
and Labor—so these are almost all assets of the

Government of Japan or the broader public sector,
not its central bank.
Korea’s National Pension Service has more foreign
assets than the Bank of Korea has FX reserves.
A different dynamic is in play in Taiwan, as the
government doesn’t run a big retirement fund. But in

### Page 4

4
a world where the hedge ratio of the life insurers has
been (and still is) used as a policy tool, the shift in
Taiwan’s foreign asset accumulation away from the
Central Bank of China (CBC) and over to
institutions whose activities in the foreign currency
market it directly influences broadly fits into the same
theme. So too does the PBOC’s use of the
“macroprudential adjustment factor” effectively adjust
banks’ net foreign assets in much the same way that

CBC regulations adjust lifer hedge ratios.
In the last year, the lifers’ hedge ratios—and their
direct hedges with the central bank—have been one
of the key tools the CBC has used to manage the
Taiwan dollar (and guard the lifers’ solvency).
Asia’s big surplus economies are in a sense just
catching up with the world’s oil exporters. Norway
put its oil surplus into a sovereign wealth fund from
the start. Kuwait, Qatar, and Abu Dhabi (the most
oil-rich emirate) all also have large sovereign funds.

And the world’s biggest oil exporter has joined them:
The Public Investment Fund has almost as many
foreign assets as the Saudi Central Bank (SAMA).*
What’s more, the available evidence suggests that the
dollar share of these pools is in line with or higher
than the dollar share of global FX reserves (it depends
on the institution and jurisdiction).

### Page 5

5
In other words, by looking at the (easily available)
data on the world’s static holdings of formal FX
reserve assets, scholars and analysts miss most of the
growth in the world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign
foreign assets.
Consider China.
No serious analyst now disputes that China’s state
banks—including the policy banks (the China
Development Bank, China Exim)—hold several
trillion in foreign assets.
China reports $3.3 trillion in gross foreign assets to

the BIS (the net position, counting foreign bank
claims on the entire Chinese economy not just the
banks, is $2.5 trillion). That maps to the BOP data,
which shows almost $4 trillion in gross outflows
through the banking system (technically, the sum of
gross outflows in “other” plus the $500 billion in
foreign currency bonds held by the state commercial
banks)

### Page 6

6
Since 2010 (and even more so after 2014) the net
outflow through the state banks has exceeded reserve
accumulation.
The broad contours of this story are confirmed by the
balance sheet data reported by state commercial
banks in their 2025 annual reports, which showed

### Page 7

7
that the top five banks held a combined $2.5 trillion
in foreign currency assets (mostly held abroad).
The Chinese haven’t disclosed the foreign assets of
the two policy banks (with at this stage the complicity
of the IMF, which has neither analyzed the role of
SAFE policy bank financing, nor highlighted the
glaring gap in China’s own reporting). But the work
of AidData points to nearly $1 trillion in foreign
assets, with a hefty dollar share.
The available data sources all suggest that the bulk of

banks—puts the dollar share of their offshore foreign
currency assets at around 70 percent. That is well
above the last disclosed dollar share of China’s formal
reserves (55 percent in 2019). The banks’ annual

### Page 8

8
reports suggest that about 70 percent of the banks’
assets are in dollars and Hong Kong dollars (55
percent USD/15 percent HKD)—a bit below the
dollar and Hong Kong dollar share of their liabilities
(swaps out of the HKD fund a decent share of the
banks euro assets).
Put simply, SAFE’s static dollar holdings aren’t the
important story.
The real story is the rapid growth of the state banks
dollar holdings.
Japan holds a high (though undisclosed) dollar share

in its FX reserves, and those reserves are primarily
invested in Treasuries—so Japan’s MoF is now clearly
the largest contributor to the U.S. data on foreign
official holdings of Treasuries. (SAFE has shifted its
funds out of U.S. custodians, and thus increasingly
appears in the data as a “private” holder in a European
custodial center)
Japan’s formal reserves haven’t changed much in the
last ten years; periodic sales to limit the yen’s weakness
have offset most of the MoF’s accumulated interest

income. Foreign currency reserves were $1.2 trillion
in 2014 and $1.16 trillion at the end of Q1. They
dipped to about $1 trillion at the end of August after
sales of close to $100 billion at the end of July/start of
August.

### Page 9

9
What has grown is the foreign portfolio of the GPIF.
it was around $400 billion in 2014, ~$1 trillion in Q1
of 2026.
GPIF’s foreign bonds aren’t held exclusively in dollars,
but roughly 52 percent of its foreign bonds (26
percent of all bonds) are USD-denominated.
But the GPIF, like most international investors, holds
a large share of its equity portfolio in the U.S. (~$165
billion, 65 percent of the foreign equity portfolio).
Korea is a more extreme version of Japan.

The Bank of Korea’s FX reserves have been roughly
$375-450 billion over the last ten years or so. Around
two-thirds of those are now in dollars (as of the end of
2025).

### Page 10

10
From 2012 to 2025, the foreign assets at the NPS
have grown from ~$80 billion (around 21 percent of
total assets) to over $660 billion (55 percent of total
assets)—and something like 70 percent of those are in
dollars. 76 percent of the fund’s equities are in dollars,
and 66 percent of its debt. And while a lower share of
its “alternative” portfolio is in North America, most
alternative mandates are awarded in dollars.

### Page 11

11
In other words, the majority of the dollar assets held
by Korea’s government (the NPS reports to the
Ministry of Health and Welfare, and the Minister of
Health chairs its asset allocation committee) aren’t
held by the central bank and aren’t in the IMF’s
COFER data set.
Taiwan is an unusual case, as the life insurers aren’t
technically sovereign investors. But with their
regulator now explicitly encouraging the them to hold
an unhedged foreign currency book and hence they

now rely on the state for their solvency, they have a bit
of an official character (a significant appreciation of
the TWD would blow through their volatility reserves
and leave them with fewer assets than liabilities, as
their foreign assets are largely held against TWD
policies). The CBC certainly now uses the lifers desire
to unwind existing hedges to help manage the Taiwan

### Page 12

12
dollar. And they have an exceptionally high dollar
share in their foreign assets—over 95 percent.
The Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF) is another
example.
It keeps roughly $200 billion of its $250 billion in
foreign investments in dollars—well above the U.S.
share of a standard global stock index. Almost all of
the PIF’s $30 billion in external debt is also in dollars.
And the PIF now has only slightly fewer foreign
assets than the Saudi Central Bank has reserves.**

The broad story is thus pretty clear: the growth in the
world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign assets is not
coming through an increase in FX reserve holdings
managed by the world’s central banks.

### Page 13

13
And, in the critical case of China, the dollar share of
China’s quasi-sovereign foreign assets is likely higher
than the dollar share of their formal FX reserves.
Adam Tooze has noted that inflows into the U.S. are
now coming from investors looking for a profit, not
investors looking for safety. What he calls the “profit
dollar” manifests itself in a number of different ways.
More inflows into corporate bonds and equities for
one. Less official demand for Treasuries for another.

More opacity too.
Those same trends are at play among sovereign and
quasi-sovereign investors.
So don’t obsess about the dollar’s chare in formal FX
reserves. Do recognize that the dollar’s “reserve
currency role” isn’t the source of any significant new
inflows into the dollar.

### Page 14

14
The dollar’s dominant role in the international
monetary system depends on much more than the
size and composition of central bank FX reserves. It is
as much, perhaps more so (given that FX reserve
managers are themselves ultimately liability matchers)
a function of the portfolio choice of a set of
private/semi-private/quasi-sovereign investors that
are much more difficult to observe. Reserve currency
status is not only about FX reserves in a strict sense.

That’s been true for some time. Most of the current
flow into U.S. from the “official” sector is coming
from investors who are not classic reserve managers.
Those flows remain heavily tilted toward the dollar, at
least for now.***
And any real de-dollarization would likely occur first
among these investors.
Put differently, the dollar’s global role is increasingly
as a source of returns, not a source of safety. The
foreign bid, private and public, is for risk, not for

Treasuries. That doesn’t help Scott Bessent much
right now, but it has helped keep valuations in the
stock market extended. And the global debate on the
dollar’s role lags the evolution in the dollar’s role, and
the risks associated with that new role.
It is currently fashionable in some circles to point to
the diminution of the “convenience yield” on

### Page 15

15
Treasuries even while the U.S. dollar continues to
enjoy such a privilege. But the shift in the global
investor base, and their apparent portfolio preference
for U.S. risk assets in lieu of Treasuries, may help
explain the divergence between the convenience yield
on UST and the convenience yield on USD.
And of course, if the negative convenience yield
implies that Treasuries are trading at a historical
discount, the persistence of the latter—and its

concentration in risk assets—suggests that the “profit
dollar” itself is likely trading at a historical premium.
That apparent premium on U.S. risk assets deserves as
much (or more) scrutiny as the currency composition
of central bank FX reserves.
* The PIF and Saudi Aramco also now have a certain
amount of debt.
** Reserves (now mostly in Treasuries, so should
appear in the TIC data, unlike at some times in the
past.

### Page 16

16

nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.

*** Japan is the main exception; the GPIF’s dollar
share is below the likely dollar share of Japan’s MoF.

### Page 3

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

3 
Summary 
The CEE region has been hit hard by a further energy shock. Yet despite the challenging 
macroeconomic environment, much of the region has shown greater resilience than 
expected. Our main article in this publication argues that the next stage of that resilience 
will depend increasingly on Outward Direct Investment (ODI), as CEE economies seek to 
evolve beyond a low-cost, FDI-to-export, strong SME-based model of growth. 
Looking at the CEE4, we discuss why the next leg of the region’s growth journey will 
involve developing successful multinationals. Countries could do more in this regard as 
their ODI remains well below the levels implied by their stage of economic development.  
So far, policymakers have focused primarily on attracting FDI and promoting exports. 
Going forward, they need to broaden this institutional framework to facilitate the foreign 
expansion of domestic firms, for example through market intelligence, access to 
financing and risk-sharing instruments, legal and regulatory support, and economic 
diplomacy. Ultimately, firms themselves need to recognise the opportunities offered by 
becoming multinational. 
This is certainly the message we are hearing from our own corporate surveys, for 
example in Poland, where foreign expansion is now seen as a necessity to break out of 
relatively small domestic markets to diversify customer bases, spread fixed costs over 
greater sales and increase the returns on investment in innovation and digitalisation. 
The Czech Republic seems to have made the most progress with ODI, while Poland and 
Romania lag. All countries could do more.  
Coming back to the immediate outlook for the region, we see a mixed picture heading 
into 2027. Poland is expected to remain one of the fastest growing economies in the EU, 
albeit with fiscal frailty. Parliamentary elections next year mean there will be little 
appetite for fiscal consolidation, and the focus will remain on the sovereign bond 
market, where downgrade risks remain and foreign holdings are low. Even though we 
expect 50bp of rate hikes locally, we see the zloty staying relatively soft. 
Hungary should enjoy an improving growth rate and a better fiscal position. The path 
towards euro adoption, which we see happening in 2032 at the earliest, would require a 
lower inflation target and a credible medium-term fiscal framework. These policy shifts 
could create room for 125bp of rate cuts by the end of 2027. Positioning on this theme is 
already crowded, but local currency bond yields should soften further and EUR/HUF 
should stay near 350/360. 
Czech activity is set to slow next year on the back of softer real wage growth, but the 
manufacturing sector has been holding up reasonably well. We see the Czech National 
Bank as one of the most hawkish in the region, having already hiked this year. At least 
another 25bp of tightening by year-end should see ongoing demand for the koruna. 
In Romania, the bond market continues to teeter on the edge of junk status. If a stable 
government can be formed, further fiscal improvement can win through and be 
rewarded with lower funding costs. This could end three years of stagnation. In Turkey, 
we are more bullish on disinflation prospects than market pricing implies. We see 700bp 
of policy easing by end 2027. However, early 2028 elections will soon come into play. 
Also covered in this publication, Croatia remains in good shape, politics remains 
challenging in Serbia, while Bulgaria is on the right path, albeit with imbalances. Ukraine 
continues to suffer from the destruction and macro imbalances of a protracted war. In 
the CIS, we see Uzbekistan as the outperformer given its strong and diversified growth, 
positive sovereign ratings momentum, and expected catch-up on gold exports.  
In short, 2027 should be a year of idiosyncratic CEE stories. We are sure that the insights 
provided by our local economists and strategists make Directional Economics an 
essential guide to the region. 
Chris Turner, Global Head of Markets and Regional Head of Research, UK & CEE

### Page 4

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

4 
Country summaries: CEE4

Czech Republic

Hungary 
The global negative supply shock has propelled inflation upward and 
is now also taking its toll on economic activity, delivering a textbook 
outcome. Renewed growth in regulated and food prices is set to 
follow punchy fuel price gains, as Hormuz second-round effects 
start to materialise. Real GDP growth is set to slow to a mediocre 
pace, while the surge in inflation is set to cap real wage growth at 
the year turn. That said, manufacturing is proving resilient and will 
lend support to economic activity. Firms’ profitability may erode due 
to rising input costs amid ample competition. The CNB is likely to opt 
for a tighter monetary policy, as the weaker koruna contributes to 
domestic price pressures via galloping import prices. Still, the currency 
is set to withstand potential bond market turmoil, as fragile French 
finances may cause the ECB to hesitate. The Czech fiscal position 
remains comparatively strong, despite some recent easing. 
 Hungary has weathered the recent market storms 
uncharacteristically well. The antidote is called the “road to the 
euro”. Policymakers have laid the foundations, and the project is 
entering its credibility-building phase. A credible medium-term fiscal 
plan, official talks on ERM II and progress towards the Maastricht 
criteria should put Hungary into a convergence corridor. At the end 
of that road, we see euro adoption in 2032 at the earliest. If all goes 
well, interest-rate convergence will follow, while keeping the forint 
stable remains a must. A 2-3% GDP growth and a 2-3% inflation 
environment in the years ahead provide solid macro foundations. 
Targeted measures will support consumption, EU funds and better 
sentiment will revive investment activity, while new export 
capacities strengthen external balances. Sovereign rating upgrades 
from 2027 would be the icing on the cake.

Poland

Romania 
Poland is set to remain one of the fastest-growing EU economies in 
2027, with public and private investment increasingly complementing 
consumption. Foreign investor exposure to equity and bonds reflects 
optimism on growth but concern on the fiscal side, with non-residents 
accounting for 80% of equity trading but only 12% of the POLGB 
stock. The government is pursuing a supply side agenda to support 
innovation in the economy, but further structural reforms are needed, 
particularly on fiscal policy, with a 2025-27 deficit of c.7% of GDP. 
We don’t see fiscal adjustment in the 2027 election year but expect 
fewer and later NBP hikes (50bp in 1Q27 vs 100bp priced). In our 
view, elevated hike expectations overstate CPI risks as they also 
reflect a deterioration of the fiscal trajectory. Hike expectations may 
quickly compress, if energy prices reverse, but fiscal risks should stay 
elevated in the coming years as the combination of 2027 election 
uncertainty and a veto prone President creates risk of a downgrade 
(1-2 notches downgrade already priced) and is PLN negative. 
 Romania’s macroeconomic adjustment is finally becoming visible, but 
progress remains vulnerable to political uncertainty. The budget deficit 
is narrowing and the country has retained its investment-grade 
rating. However, all three major rating agencies continue to rate 
Romania at the lowest investment grade with negative outlooks, 
leaving little room for policy missteps. The political stalemate risks 
overshadowing fiscal progress and keeping the sovereign risk 
premium elevated. With the economy quasi-stagnated for almost 
three years, further consolidation will be difficult. A broad political 
agreement on the 2027-28 fiscal framework would be the key 
positive catalyst, improving policy visibility, supporting the rating 
outlook and allowing fiscal adjustment to translate more clearly into 
lower funding costs and a gradual recovery in confidence. 
At the time of writing (12 October), a new government is expected 
to be installed in the coming days, with a parliamentary 
confidence vote likely on 20 October.

Country summaries: Other Central & Eastern Europe

Bulgaria

Croatia 
Bulgaria enters the post-euro period with a stronger institutional 
and financial anchor, but also with a less comfortable 
macroeconomic mix. Domestic demand remains strong, supported 
by rapid wage growth, a tight labour market and robust public 
investments. At the same time, elevated inflation, accelerating 
credit and widening twin deficits expose the trade-offs from an 
increasingly procyclical policy stance. The alignment of bank reserve 
requirements with euro-area standards has also improved domestic 
funding conditions, supporting financial deepening but increasing 
the importance of macroprudential policy as credit growth and 
property prices accelerate. Low public debt remains an important 
buffer, but recurring expenditure commitments are gradually 
narrowing the margin for error. 
 Croatia’s economy continues to outperform the euro area, supported 
by resilient consumption, a tight labour market and the final phase 
of RRF-funded investment. However, growth has slowed more visibly 
in 2026, as sticky inflation, rising labour costs and weaker tourism 
have taken their toll on activity. Fiscal policy remains supportive, 
although strong nominal revenues should keep the deficit close to 
3% of GDP and allow public debt to decline gradually. At the same 
time, a widening current-account deficit highlights the growing gap 
between still-solid domestic demand and the economy’s external 
earning capacity. Political stability and a solid sovereign rating 
remain important buffers, while a key medium-term objective 
resides in the rotation of the RRF-driven investment and cohesion 
funding towards greater private investment.

Serbia

Turkey 
Serbia’s economy has remained resilient despite elevated domestic 
and external uncertainty, supported by robust consumption, a 
strong labour market and continued investment ahead of EXPO 
2027. The more supportive fiscal stance, including higher social 
spending, should sustain near-term demand but could add to both 
inflation and external pressures. The unresolved NIS ownership issue 
remains the main energy risk, as the US Treasury’s extension lasts 
only until 30 October. Meanwhile, the 25 October parliamentary 
election and Aleksandar Vučić’s pursuit of the premiership add to 
the near-term political uncertainty, while Serbia’s long-term 
balancing of relations with the EU, Russia and China has become 
increasingly difficult against the current geopolitical backdrop. 
 The Middle East remains focused on the US-Iran standoff with 
pressure on energy prices creating challenges for net energy-
importing economies, such as Turkey. Domestically, the closure of 
certain investment funds prompted some investors to unwind 
money market fund positions, although dollarization pressures 
remained contained. CBT reserves declined in September amid 
weaker confidence, while maintaining strong reserve buffers 
remains important for absorbing potential shocks and preserving 
the exchange rate as a key policy anchor. Slower economic activity, 
softer domestic demand, low core goods inflation and easing 
services inflation have reduced upside inflation risks, leading the CBT 
to lower the effective funding cost. Markets continue to expect 
further rate cuts this year, although reserve volatility and investor 
confidence remain important considerations for the CBT.

### Page 5

5 
Country summaries: CIS

Armenia

Azerbaijan  
Armenia has shown remarkable resilience to the global and regional 
challenges of 2026, supported by renewed inflows of visitors, 
remittances and capital. Skilful foreign policy manoeuvring and solid 
finances provide additional support to sentiment and insulation to 
the balance of payments. However, the increasing reliance on 
external inflows is a watch factor, as persistent twin deficits and 
close economic links with Russia remain important vulnerabilities. 
Higher CPI risks stemming from domestic demand and external cost 
inputs have prompted the region’s first rate hike this year, with 
further upside risks in sight. While we remain constructive on 
Armenia’s macro outlook and assets, we see growing exposure to 
foreign politics-related downside risks, while the AMD appears 
increasingly expensive relative to fundamentals. 
 Azerbaijan remains the CCA region’s financially strongest sovereign, 
supported by large and growing fiscal and monetary reserve assets, 
low public debt and a robust external balance sheet. Higher 
hydrocarbon prices have strengthened the near-term fiscal and 
external outlook, as well as domestic confidence in the non-oil 
sectors. However, weak oil production, limited diversification and 
subdued private investment continue to constrain potential growth. 
Inflation is manageable for now, supporting an extended policy 
hold, although imported price pressures through high exposure to 
trading partners with high risks of upward CPI surprises, warrant 
caution. We remain constructive on sovereign resilience and 
external stability but see structurally modest growth as the main 
medium-term challenge.

Kazakhstan

Ukraine 
Kazakhstan continues to benefit from one of the strongest 
sovereign and external positions in the CCA region, supported by 
sizeable sovereign assets, a balanced network of international 
partners and higher oil prices. More recently, strong portfolio inflows 
given still-attractive real rates, progress towards Euroclear 
connectivity, growing domestic debt supply and positive rating 
momentum have supported the tenge and local markets. In 
addition, a sizeable backlog of future state FX sales provides an 
important buffer against potential market stress. At the same time, 
the longer-term outlook remains constrained by persistent twin 
deficits, limited private-sector investment and capital inflows and an 
uneven track record in fiscal discipline and inflation targeting. 
Current CPI trend leaves room for a moderate easing, but the 
trajectory is facing upside risks. 
 The hryvnia remained under pressure as the dollar strengthened, 
partly due to renewed Middle East tensions. Consequently, USD/UAH 
temporarily broke above 45.0, reaching its highest level on record. 
Hryvnia depreciation could have been sharper, but NBU actions 
provided support. In September, the central bank raised rates by 
50bp to 16.0% and maintained sizeable FX interventions, with the 
four-week average close to record highs, which demonstrates the 
NBU’s determination to stabilise the exchange rate. FX reserves 
remain volatile but new tranches of foreign loans should lift them to 
around US$60bn. The macroeconomic environment remains 
challenging due to the ongoing war with Russia, despite intensified 
rhetoric and pressure from the US administration.

Uzbekistan

Uzbekistan remains our preferred story in the CCA region, 
combining strong and diversified growth, continued reforms and 
positive sovereign-credit momentum. An expected catch-up in gold 
exports, alongside ongoing capital inflows linked to the privatisation 
pipeline (UzNIF+12 SOEs) is supporting a positive performance from 
the soum. Combined with fiscal consolidation, this has helped bring 
the inflation rate down since the start of the year, improving the 
scope for further monetary easing. At the same time, longer-term 
vulnerabilities stem from persisting twin deficits, continued high 
state involvement in the economy and a long history of above-
target inflation. A more meaningful monetary easing and a lasting 
break from UZS’s depreciation trend would require a further decline 
in inflation and more durable private capital inflows.

ING EM FX and local rates views summary

Country Foreign exchange Local debt and rates 
Czech Republic EUR/CZK has remained stable since the onset of the US-Iran 
conflict and, looking ahead, the koruna should be supported by 
a hawkish tone from the CNB. An additional rate hike in 
November, along with the risk of further hikes and rising 
inflation through to the end of the year, should push the pair 
back towards 24.00, with the koruna to outperform its CEE peers. 
The hawkish CNB, combined with significant inflation exposure to 
energy and food prices, should sustain aggressive market pricing, 
even in the event of a global relief, given the prevailing inflationary 
environment. Likely exclusion from the GBI-EM index (a benchmark 
for local-currency emerging-market government bonds) supports 
a bearish outlook for CZGBs. 
Hungary The Hungarian story following the general election in April is 
unfolding according to plan; persistently high interest rates and 
the market expectation that the NBH will not cut rates should 
keep the forint supported. While global factors and heavy long 
positioning run counter to bullish sentiment, we still expect the 
EUR/HUF pair to grind lower from here. 
Bonds and rates have seen a significant rally and country premium 
compression since the general election, and we remain bullish on 
the local story. However, Hungary was our best trade this year; 
given tight valuations and crowded positioning, we are waiting for 
better levels and looking to fade sell-offs given that compression is 
ahead of the usual schedule of euro adoption.  
Poland Poland remains exposed to the global market, not only due to 
energy prices but also because of a record-high fiscal deficit. 
The central bank is moving closer to rate hikes, yet it leans 
towards the dovish side with the widest gap between market 
expectations and our own forecast for the key policy rate path. 
This combination should push the EUR/PLN pair higher. 
A record-high public finance deficit, rising inflation and the risk of 
further rating downgrades could generate significant selling 
pressure for POLGBs. The central bank is likely to continue delaying 
rate hikes. We expect the first rate hike in 1Q27, with rate hikes still 
priced in for this year.  
Romania  EUR/RON has experienced increased volatility in recent weeks, 
but the outlook regarding the new government should help 
stabilise the pair. On the other hand, we see no room for a 
downside. The risk remains tilted to the upside should political 
uncertainty return, the government disappoint the market 
regarding fiscal consolidation or the rating get downgraded.  
ROMGBs have seen a significant tightening of spreads relative to 
CEE peers and valuations appear expensive. The issuance and fiscal 
outlook remain uncertain, while the credit rating outlook clouds 
the future. A rebound in inflation complicates the outlook for NBR 
rate cuts.  
Ukraine  The hryvnia remained under pressure as the dollar 
strengthened, partly due to renewed Middle East tensions. 
Consequently, USD/UAH temporarily broke above 45.0, reaching 
its highest level on record. The NBU raised rates by 50bp to 
16.0% and maintained sizeable FX interventions, which 
demonstrates the NBU’s determination to stabilise the FX. 
Substantial external financial assistance in the form of foreign 
loans from international partners has translated into fast rising 
public and external debt levels, both of which exceeded 100% of 
GDP in 2025, and are set to rise further in the coming years. Both 
fiscal and external current account gaps, as well as public and 
external debt levels, are set to widen significantly. 
Kazakhstan The easing in inflation and a resilient tenge have allowed the 
NBK to start cutting the base rate in 2026. Higher oil prices have 
strengthened the external position. We see an improved KZT 
outlook but note that greater reliance on portfolio capital in an 
economy with persistent twin deficits also increases sensitivity 
to shifts in global risk appetite and flow reversals.

Turkey  The CBT is leaving less carry on the table for TRY longs, yet we 
also see the central bank’s commitment to maintaining the 
current FX regime for longer. FX reserves remain near highs and 
the recent funds crisis has once again demonstrated the 
stability of the FX rate during periods of stress.  
In line with our expectations, the market is pricing in 200bp of rate 
cuts from the CBT; however, for next year, it anticipates rates to 
remain roughly stable, whereas our forecast points to further cuts. 
The curve should see more steepening, given underpriced CBT 
expectations, and duration buyers would need to see greater 
global relief before returning to the market, which is not on horizon.

Central Europe’s next growth engine:  
From FDI hosts to global investors 
Introduction 
Central and Eastern Europe (CEE) has been remarkably successful in attracting foreign 
companies. The next challenge is to build more multinationals of its own. For three 
decades, inward foreign direct investment (FDI) and exports have powered the region’s 
integration with Western Europe, bringing technology and know-how, which supported 
rapid income convergence (see 2024 Directional: Why beating the middle-income trap 
isn’t enough). But as labour-cost advantages gradually diminish (see 2025 Directional: 
From working hard to working smart), the old growth model will become harder to 
sustain. The next stage of convergence will increasingly require successful domestic 
firms to scale internationally. 
Outward direct investment (ODI) can become an important new engine of CEE growth. 
Investing abroad gives firms access to new customers, technologies, brands and 
capabilities. It allows successful companies to overcome the limitations of relatively 
small domestic markets, achieve greater scale and strengthen their ability to innovate. 
Profits generated abroad can also become an additional source of national income. 
CEE has already started this transition, but the region still punches below its weight 
as a foreign investor. Czechia has progressed furthest, while ODI in Poland and Romania 
remains low relative to their level of economic development. This does not appear to 
reflect a shortage of firms capable of competing internationally: the region has created 
a growing group of successful companies with the capital, technology and managerial 
capabilities required to expand abroad. What many still lack is sufficient scale, 
experience and an institutional environment that makes foreign investment easier. 
Economic policy needs to adjust to support ODI. For decades, CEE governments have 
concentrated on attracting foreign investors and helping domestic companies export. 
This is gradually changing, but support for outward investment remains less developed. 
Governments do not need to pick national champions or subsidise capital leaving the 
country. Their role should be to remove unnecessary barriers by providing market 
intelligence, financing and risk-sharing instruments, regulatory support and effective 
economic diplomacy. 
Ultimately, firms rather than governments will drive the transition. Entrepreneurs and 
managers must identify viable opportunities, build international capabilities and accept 
the risks associated with expansion abroad. Public institutions can make that process 
easier, but they cannot substitute for business decisions. CEE's next development 
challenge is therefore clear: to remain an attractive home for global companies while 
creating the conditions for more of its own firms to become global. 
Investment Development Path framework 
The Investment Development Path (IDP) framework implies CEE should invest abroad. 
The IDP, formulated by Dunning,0F
1 links a country's ODI and FDI stocks to its stage of 
development. The central idea of the IDP is that as countries become wealthier and 
more competitive, they evolve from being primarily hosts for foreign investment to 
becoming significant outward investors. As discussed in a recent survey of Sawitri and

1 Dunning J., 1981. Explaining the international direct investment position of countries: Towards a dynamic or 
developmental approach Weltwirtschaftliches Archiv 117, 30 –64.

Michał Rubaszek 
Senior Economist, Poland 
michal.rubaszek@ing.pl 
1

### Page 9

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

9 
Brennan,1F
2 the IDP framework is the most frequently employed empirical assessment 
reflecting a country’s direct investment activities in relation to the level of economic 
development.  
The IDP model identifies five stages of economic development: 
Stage I. A low-income country with insignificant levels of both FDI and ODI.  
Stage II. A developing country that becomes an attractive localization for multinationals. 
Active government policies promote FDI, which stock increases to significant levels. ODI 
remains low and the net position is deteriorating.  
Stage III. A middle income country, in which firms have already accumulated 
technological and managerial capabilities. The government actively promote FDI and 
exports, with little focus on ODI. FDI stabilizes at high levels and ODI starts to emerge. 
Stage IV. A high-middle income country with emerging mass of competitive domestic 
firms that become multinationals, which is supported by the government. FDI stabilizes 
and ODI grows so that net position is improving. 
Stage V. A developed economy with extensive multinational activity. The government 
plays a less significant role in attracting FDI or stimulating ODI. The levels of FDI and ODI 
stocks are stable at high levels with balanced or positive net position. 
Fig 1 IDP framework for the link between FDI/ODI stocks and economic development

Source: ING based on Sawitri and Brennan (2023)

The IDP model is a suitable framework for aggregate analysis as it perceives FDI and 
ODI as both a consequence and a driver of economic growth. Inward foreign 
investments enhance productivity, technology transfer, and competitiveness, while 
outward investments reflect the emergence of productive domestic firms capable of 
exploiting their advantages internationally. The exact trajectory varies across countries 
depending on their institutions, policies, industrial structure, and integration into the 
global economy. The extensions of the IDP framework emphasize these country-specific 
factors and the increasing complexity of multinational enterprise strategies in a 
globalized economy (see Fig 1).  
Progress of CEE within Investment Development Path 
The evolution of FDI and ODI in CEE conforms to the predictions of the IDP framework. 
Fig 2 illustrates that in the mid-1990s CEE economies (Czechia, Hungary, Poland, 
Romania) were in Stage I of the IDP, as the stocks of both FDI and ODI were relatively

2 Sawitri, K., Brennan, L., 2023. The investment development path literature: a review and research agenda. 
Management Review Quarterly 73: 1825–1872. 
2

### Page 10

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

10 
insignificant. Between 1995 and 2010, they progressed towards Stage II as the 
substantial inflow of FDI was accompanied by limited ODI. During this period, FDI inflows 
were supported by active government policies, including tax incentives and subsidies, 
combined with the stability and credibility provided by the EU accession process. By 
2010, the stock of inward FDI ranged from around 40% of GDP in Romania to nearly 70% 
of GDP in Hungary, whereas ODI stocks remained much lower. Subsequently, in years 
2010-2025, CEE4 progressed towards Stage III of the IDP: FDI stocks stabilized at 
relatively high levels, while ODI began to emerge. At present, one can observe the 
increasing number of internationally active domestic firms, which signals a transition 
towards Stage IV of the IDP.  
Fig 2 The evolution of FDI and ODI in years 1995-2025 (stocks, as % GDP) 
0BCzech Republic 1BHungary

2BPoland 3BRomania

Source: UNCTAD World Investment Report, IMF WEO, ING calculations

CEE4 are heterogeneous in their progress along the IDP. Fig 2 indicates that Czechia 
and Hungary have succeeded in attracting large FDI stocks, well in excess of 50% of GDP, 
while simultaneously expanding ODI stocks to levels above 20% of GDP. It must be 
noted, however, that about three quarters of Hungarian ODI stock are related to special 
purpose entities (SPEs), which substantially distort the picture of cross-border 
investment. If SPEs are not accounted for, the level of Hungarian ODI falls to about 5% of 
GDP.2F
3  Poland and Romania, the two largest CEE4 countries, recorded FDI stocks 
fluctuating around 40% of GDP and ODI remaining below 5% of GDP. Overall, while all 
CEE4 economies have successfully attracted FDI, only Czechia has made significant 
progress towards Stage IV of the IDP. Low levels of ODI in Hungary, Poland and Romania 
indicates that the internationalization of domestic firms remains an important 
challenge.

3 SPEs are legal entities with little or no production or operations typically established to channel funds within 
multinational groups (see Eurostat).

Fig 3 ODI stock and the level of economic development in 2025

Source: UNCTAD World Investment Report, IMF WEO, ING calculations

ODI levels in CEE remain below those implied by their level of economic development. 
We illustrate this in Fig 3, which presents the relationship between the stock of ODI as a 
share of GDP and GDP per capita for a sample of global economies. The fitted trend 
curve suggests that at GDP per capita levels of 40,000–50,000 PPS, ODI stocks would 
typically amount to around 20% of GDP. The positions of Poland and Romania indicate 
that their ODI stocks remain low relative to what might be expected given their level of 
economic development. By contrast, ODI in Czechia is close to the fitted trend, while 
Hungary lies above it, although this result is largely driven by the activity of SPEs. A 
comparison with Portugal and Spain, two EU countries with broadly comparable levels of 
GDP per capita, further highlights this gap, as both display substantially higher ODI 
stocks than the CEE economies. Figure 3 also suggests that, as income convergence 
continues, ODI stocks in the CEE should increase towards the levels observed in more 
advanced economies, where they typically range between 30% and 60% of GDP.

CEE outward investments remain strongly concentrated in Europe. Investing abroad is 
challenging for CEE companies due to numerous factors, including insufficient access to 
capital, limited public support, and relatively little experience in establishing and 
managing foreign affiliates compared with Western European firms. CEE firms are also 
at a disadvantage relative to companies from selected Western European countries that 
can profit long-standing linguistic, cultural and institutional ties with geographically 
distant markets. Spain and Portugal provide particularly relevant examples, as their 
historical links and shared languages with Latin America have facilitated ODI in this 
region. These differences are reflected in the geographical structure of ODI. Fig 4 
illustrates that most investment from the CEE is located in Europe, often in neighbouring 
countries, where geographical and cultural proximity, established trade relationships 
and familiarity with local business conditions reduce the costs and risks of establishing 
foreign affiliates. In 2024, the share of investment outside Europe in total ODI amounted 
to merely 7.2% in Czechia, 11.5% in Poland, 22.3% in Romania and 47.6% in Hungary. 
However, once special purpose entities (SPEs) are excluded, the corresponding share for 
Hungary declines to 11.6%. These figures are substantially below the 35.0% share of 
extra-European investment in total ODI originating from EU countries.  
Moving towards stage IV of IDP should boost growth 
Moving towards Stage IV of the IDP constitutes an upgrade of the CEE growth model. 
Progress towards Stage IV does not mean abandoning the successful model from the 
past, but complementing it with a stronger domestic multinational sector. Instead of 
relying predominantly on foreign firms to bring technology, capital and know-how, an 
increasing number of CEE firms could themselves exploit their competitive advantages 
abroad. Rising ODI should be viewed not simply as capital leaving the domestic 
economy, but as evidence that domestic firms have accumulated sufficient capabilities 
to compete, invest and generate income internationally. 
The economic benefits of this transition could be substantial for firms. Domestic 
markets, even in Poland and Romania, are relatively small compared with the global 
market, placing limits on the scale that successful firms can achieve domestically. As 
enumerated by Gorynia et al.,3F
4 expanding abroad allows firms to enlarge their customer 
base, spread fixed costs over greater sales, improve asset utilisation and increase the 
returns to expenditure on innovation, automation, digitalisation and brand 
development. Foreign investment can therefore help successful CEE firms move from 
competing predominantly on costs towards competing increasingly on scale, 
technology, brands and other firm-specific advantages. In other words, at the firm level, 
ODI should support the transition towards productivity and innovation driven expansion 
model.  
ODI brings benefits to the home economy through several channels. Foreign affiliates 
provide access to new customers, hence can strengthen exports by creating distribution 
networks and generating demand for intermediate goods and services supplied by the 
parent company and its domestic suppliers. Foreign acquisitions may provide access to 
technologies, brands, managerial expertise and other strategic assets that can 
subsequently be transferred across the multinational group. Greater international scale 
can also strengthen firms’ incentives to invest at home in research and development. 
Finally, profits earned abroad add to national income and diversify corporate earnings 
away from domestic economic conditions. These channels are particularly relevant for 
the relatively small and open CEE economies.

4 Gorynia, M., Nowak, J., Wolniak, R., Trąpczyński, P. (2022). How does economic integration affect progress along 
the investment development path? A case study of EU member vs. non -member countries from Eastern Europe. 
Journal of Modern Science, 49(2), 177-214. 
3

### Page 13

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

13 
Increasing ODI should not be interpreted as an objective for its own sake. Not all ODI 
benefits the home economy. ODI may involve the relocation of production, employment 
or investment abroad, particularly when firms invest primarily to lower production costs. 
It may therefore substitute for domestic capital formation or adversely affect some 
domestic suppliers and workers. Foreign operations also expose companies to additional 
commercial, regulatory, political and exchange-rate risks. The balance of these effects 
depends on the motivation and type of investment and, in particular, on whether foreign 
operations complement or substitute for domestic activities.  
Fig 5 Channels of transmission from ODI to home economy

Source: ING based on Knoerich and Benson (2026)

Empirical evidence is clear that ODI is beneficial for home countries. The recent survey 
of 130 academic studies by Knoerich and Benson 4F
5 reveals that most studies report 
positive effects of ODI for aggregate economic activity, where the effects operate 
through numerous channels of transmission (see Fig 5). They indicate that by expanding 
internationally, firms can reach larger markets, acquire technologies and other strategic 
assets, and improve their competitiveness, with part of these benefits subsequently 
transmitted back to the home economy. The evidence also suggests that the benefits 
tend to be stronger when firms invest in advanced economies, seek strategic assets 
such as technology, and possess sufficient capacity to absorb knowledge acquired 
abroad. For CEE economies, at their current stage of development, potential benefits 
of ODI are likely to outweigh their costs. 
Conditions of moving towards Stage IV of IDP 
There are two determinants of firm’s decision about foreign investment: productivity 
and fixed costs of investing abroad. This is the key implication of the Helpman-Melitz-
Yeaple (HMY) model,5F
6 in which less productive firms serve only the domestic market, 
more productive firms become exporters, and the most productive firms undertake 
direct investment abroad. The productivity thresholds are determined by fixed costs of 
going global. The model clearly implies that, once a sufficient number of highly 
productive firms emerges, policies aimed at reducing the fixed costs of foreign 
expansion should increase the scale of ODI. This insight is particularly relevant for CEE 
economies. The region's most competitive firms appear increasingly to possess the 
technological capabilities, managerial expertise, financial resources, and international

Review 94 (1): 300–316.

market knowledge required to undertake profitable investments abroad. Consequently, 
policies designed to lower barriers of entering foreign markets could play an important 
role in accelerating the transition of CEE economies towards the stage IV of the IDP. 
HMY model is strongly supported by firm-level data: internationally active companies 
are substantially larger and more productive than firms operating exclusively in 
domestic markets. At the EU level, the average employment in domestic firms is 4.2 
employees, compared with 72.0 in foreign-owned firms and 125.9 in foreign affiliates of 
domestic companies. Similarly, average turnover per employee amounts to €199 
thousand in domestic firms, compared with €444 thousand in foreign-owned firms and 
€406 thousand in foreign affiliates. These numbers illustrate the strong association 
between internationalisation, scale and productivity. Expanding the group of CEE firms 
capable of operating across borders could therefore contribute to aggregate productivity 
growth by allowing the region's most competitive companies to reach a considerably 
greater scale.

The example of Asian Tigers proves that limiting fixed entry costs boosts ODI, which is 
the key mechanism emphasized by the HMY model . The main logic of ODI policies in 
Asian Tigers is that domestic firms must expand abroad to overcome the limitations of 
domestic market, which can be facilitated by reducing informational and organizational 
barriers associated with establishing foreign affiliates. In Singapore, a dedicated entity 
EnterpriseSG has been established to help companies in scaling up by going global 
through exports and ODI. EnterpriseSG supervise 35 centers in over 20 countries, each 
providing market research, database of foreign partners, and legal assistance to 
Singaporean companies in establishing foreign subsidiaries. EnterpriseSG also offers 
financing schemes that support firms' internationalization through ODI. In South Korea, 
ODI expansion has been also been facilitated by a dedicated agency: the Korea Trade-
Investment Promotion Agency (KOTRA). It now operates 131 offices in 86 countries to 
assist Korean firms entering foreign markets by providing market intelligence, business 
networks, diplomatic and legal support. The policy focus is less on subsidizing ODI 
directly but on alleviating information and coordination failures associated with 
internationalization. The Asian Tigers experience also implies that governments are 
effective when they focus on lowering the fixed costs of foreign market entry, rather 
than attempting to directly determine which firms and where should invest.  
ODI promotion by CEE institutions 
ODI promotion in CEE countries remains relatively underdeveloped and is usually 
embedded within broader strategies for promoting exports and attracting FDI. This 
largely reflects the historical focus of CEE countries on attracting inward FDI and 
supporting exports rather than explicitly encouraging outward investment. However, 
there are signs that this model of internationalisation is gradually evolving towards a 
broader approach that increasingly recognizes ODI as an additional channel for the 
5

### Page 15

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

15 
international expansion of domestic firms. This shift can build on existing institutions, 
which already support firms operating abroad by providing market intelligence, 
financing, risk mitigation, and general advisory services. 
In Poland, the institutional framework for internationalisation has traditionally 
focused predominantly on export promotion and attracting inward FDI, although it is 
gradually evolving towards broader support for the foreign expansion of Polish firms. 
The Polish Investment and Trade Agency (PAIH) is the main agency responsible for 
internationalization of the economy, as it maintains a global network of nearly 60 
Foreign Trade Offices, covering more than 70 foreign markets. Historically, PAIH has 
been strongly oriented towards supporting exporters and attracting foreign investors to 
Poland, and these objectives remain prominent in its 2025–2029 strategy. However, the 
new strategy also envisages supporting foreign expansion of Polish firms by providing 
market intelligence and advisory support. A more explicit focus on ODI is presented in 
the Polish Development Fund (PFR) strategy for 2026–2030, in which it identifies the 
internationalisation of Polish firms as one of its strategic priorities, covering both exports 
and ODI. PFR's strategy explicitly envisages investment in the international expansion of 
Polish companies. In 2015 PFR launched the first edition of Foreign Expansion Fund (FEF), 
which in the period 2015-2023 co-financed about 20 ODI projects with the total sum of 
€100 million. The second edition of FEF, which was initiated in 2023, aims to co-invests 
with Polish firms in foreign affiliates by providing co-financing for acquisitions, greenfield 
investments, and the expansion of existing foreign operations. The engagements is not 
large and range from EUR 3 million to EUR 20 million and may cover up to 50% of total 
project value. Finally, KUKE complements these instruments by providing insurance and 
risk-mitigation instruments for Polish firms investing abroad. Taken together, these 
developments indicate a gradual shift from a framework centred on exports and FDI 
towards a broader internationalisation strategy that increasingly recognizes ODI. 
Nevertheless, dedicated support for ODI remains relatively modest compared with the 
scale of instruments available for exporters and inward investors. 
In Czechia ODI promotion is a side effect of exports promotion. CzechTrade is the main 
agency supporting Czech firms expansion abroad. The agency maintains a network of 
foreign offices in more than 50 countries, providing local market intelligence and 
facilitating contacts with potential business partners. The Czech model remains strongly 
export-oriented, but many of the services offered by CzechTrade, especially partner 
searches, market studies, and foreign offices, can also support ODI decisions. Czech firms 
contemplating acquisitions or greenfield investments abroad often rely on the same 
informational infrastructure initially developed for exporters.  Overall, Czech policy 
focuses primarily on export support rather than dedicated ODI promotion, but provides a 
substantial institutional platform lowering the information costs of foreign expansion. 
In Hungary ODI support is linked to National Export Strategy. The Hungarian Export 
Promotion Agency (HEPA), which operates under the supervision of the Ministry of 
Foreign Affairs and Trade, is responsible for promoting the international expansion of 
Hungarian enterprises through exports, but also investments abroad. HEPA reduces the 
fixed costs of foreign-market entry by providing market intelligence and advisory 
services, i.a. by collaborating with network of foreign economic attachés and overseas 
offices. International expansion is also supported by EXIM Hungary, whose programs 
include preferential financing, insurance and guarantee products for foreign expansion. 
However, the system remains predominantly export-oriented rather than explicitly 
focused on supporting ODI. 
Romania also remains focused on exports and attracting inward FDI. The Romanian 
Agency for Investments and Foreign Trade (ARICE) is the key responsible for supporting 
the internationalization of domestic firms. Its instruments include the Export Promotion

### Page 16

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

16 
Programme, international trade fairs and economic missions, business matchmaking, 
and information on investment opportunities abroad. The support remains 
predominantly oriented towards exports and initial foreign-market entry, while 
dedicated instruments addressing the financing and risk-sharing needs of firms 
undertaking ODI appear underdeveloped. 
Overall, CEE countries have developed institutions supporting the internationalization of 
domestic firms, but these institutions remain primarily oriented towards export 
promotion rather than ODI. Poland's PAIH, Czechia's CzechTrade, Hungary's HEPA, and 
Romania's ARICE all provide market intelligence, business matchmaking, and foreign-
office networks that reduce the fixed costs of entering foreign markets. However, 
dedicated financial instruments supporting ODI remain limited. In our view, current 
internationalization policies in CEE insufficiently recognize the benefits of ODI. 
CEE firms already go multinational 
CEE firms are steadily expanding their presence abroad, suggesting that they have 
developed the capabilities required for ODI. CEE firms have already accumulated 
capital, technology, managerial expertise and knowledge of foreign markets so that the 
most productive firms increasingly progress beyond exports towards a permanent 
presence abroad through acquisitions, greenfield investment and launching foreign 
subsidiaries. This evolution is visible in the growing number and scale of foreign affiliates 
controlled by CEE companies. Fig 7 displays that between 2016 and 2023, the number of 
foreign affiliates owned by CEE4 firms increased by 34% (from 2,746 to 3,670). This 
growth was particularly strong in Czechia (from 233 to 807) and Romania (from 101 to 
210), and somewhat less pronounced in Poland (from 2,151 to 2,325) and Hungary 
(from 261 to 328). The expansion of foreign affiliates has been accompanied by an even 
stronger increase in their employment. Between 2016 and 2023, employment in foreign 
affiliates controlled by CEE4 firms rose from 182 thousand to 290 thousand, i.e. by 60%. 
This points to foreign expansion along both the extensive margin, through a larger 
network of affiliates, and the intensive margin, through the expansion foreign affiliate 
size.
